Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.

And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.

I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I’ll mean to, and then I get distracted or it feels like too much trouble, and suddenly it’s late and I realize I haven’t eaten. Then I’ll just have toast or a bowl of cereal, if that. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this overwhelming sense of failure and hopelessness before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve felt I messed things up before—everybody has, I suppose—but this is different.\n\nIt’s like… I came in because I can’t get away from this feeling that I failed as a father, and failed at being any kind of useful person. It just loops around in my head. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. So… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway—had this little station I was building, tiny benches and everything—and it would kind of settle me down. Now it’s just sitting there half-finished on the table.\n\nAnd Cubs games too… my daughter and I used to watch them together, even when they were terrible, which, you know, they often are. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.

It’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.

So I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.

I don’t know… that felt different. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.

It’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I can’t get away from this feeling that I’ve failed. As a father, mostly, and just as a useful person in general. It sort of loops in my head.\n\nI was sitting at home the other day looking at this old toolbox I never use anymore—funny what you notice when you’re just sitting there—and I kept thinking, what good am I to anyone now? And then it goes from there to feeling like maybe I don’t really deserve much of a future. That’s… that’s why I’m here, I think.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about failing as a father and a person?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I knew she was going—of course I knew—but the house got so quiet, and I keep noticing little things she left behind, like a mug or a sweatshirt, and then I start thinking I didn’t do enough, or I somehow messed it all up. So… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway—had this little station I was building, tiny benches and everything—and it would kind of settle me down. Now it’s just sitting there half-finished on the table.\n\nAnd Cubs games too… my daughter and I used to watch them together, even when they were terrible, which, you know, they often are. But now I’ll turn the TV on, or even just think about putting a game on, and it feels pointless. Like I can’t get into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt’s strange, because mornings used to be when I’d make coffee and check the paper—well, online now, I guess, though I still miss the actual newspaper—and lately I catch myself thinking, what if I just didn’t have to do another one of these days.\n\nAnd what really scared me was I found myself going through my finances, sort of organizing things, like I was getting it ready for someone else to handle. I don’t know… that felt different. It made me think maybe I shouldn’t just keep this to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think of something, but… not really. I used to look forward to the model railway, you know, fiddling with the little tracks and buildings—there’s one train just sitting there half-built on the table, and I can’t seem to make myself care about finishing it.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch those together, make jokes about the announcers and all that. Now even turning a game on feels… pointless. Like it’s just noise in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I’ll mean to, and then I get distracted or it feels like too much trouble, and suddenly it’s late and I realize I haven’t eaten. Then I’ll just have toast or a bowl of cereal, if that. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this overwhelming sense of failure and hopelessness before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve felt I messed things up before—everybody has, I suppose—but this is different.\n\nIt’s like… I came in because I can’t get away from this feeling that I failed as a father, and failed at being any kind of useful person. It just loops around in my head. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It sort of loops in my head.\n\nI was sitting at home the other day looking at this old toolbox I never use anymore—funny what you notice when you’re just sitting there—and I kept thinking, what good am I to anyone now? And then it goes from there to feeling like maybe I don’t really deserve much of a future. That’s… that’s why I’m here, I think.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about failing as a father and a person?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I knew she was going—of course I knew—but the house got so quiet, and I keep noticing little things she left behind, like a mug or a sweatshirt, and then I start thinking I didn’t do enough, or I somehow messed it all up. So… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway—had this little station I was building, tiny benches and everything—and it would kind of settle me down. Now it’s just sitting there half-finished on the table.\n\nAnd Cubs games too… my daughter and I used to watch them together, even when they were terrible, which, you know, they often are. But now I’ll turn the TV on, or even just think about putting a game on, and it feels pointless. Like I can’t get into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt’s strange, because mornings used to be when I’d make coffee and check the paper—well, online now, I guess, though I still miss the actual newspaper—and lately I catch myself thinking, what if I just didn’t have to do another one of these days.\n\nAnd what really scared me was I found myself going through my finances, sort of organizing things, like I was getting it ready for someone else to handle. I don’t know… that felt different. It made me think maybe I shouldn’t just keep this to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think of something, but… not really. I used to look forward to the model railway, you know, fiddling with the little tracks and buildings—there’s one train just sitting there half-built on the table, and I can’t seem to make myself care about finishing it.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch those together, make jokes about the announcers and all that. Now even turning a game on feels… pointless. Like it’s just noise in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I’ll mean to, and then I get distracted or it feels like too much trouble, and suddenly it’s late and I realize I haven’t eaten. Then I’ll just have toast or a bowl of cereal, if that. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. And then it goes from there to feeling like maybe I don’t really deserve much of a future. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I knew she was going—of course I knew—but the house got so quiet, and I keep noticing little things she left behind, like a mug or a sweatshirt, and then I start thinking I didn’t do enough, or I somehow messed it all up. So… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway—had this little station I was building, tiny benches and everything—and it would kind of settle me down. Now it’s just sitting there half-finished on the table.\n\nAnd Cubs games too… my daughter and I used to watch them together, even when they were terrible, which, you know, they often are. But now I’ll turn the TV on, or even just think about putting a game on, and it feels pointless. Like I can’t get into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt’s strange, because mornings used to be when I’d make coffee and check the paper—well, online now, I guess, though I still miss the actual newspaper—and lately I catch myself thinking, what if I just didn’t have to do another one of these days.\n\nAnd what really scared me was I found myself going through my finances, sort of organizing things, like I was getting it ready for someone else to handle. I don’t know… that felt different. It made me think maybe I shouldn’t just keep this to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think of something, but… not really. Like it’s just noise in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.\n\nIt’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode of depression like this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s hard to separate the actual feeling from all the bits around it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll remember a room I was sitting in, or some stupid errand I kept putting off, and then I lose the thread of what you asked.\n\nBut… no, I can’t point to another time and say, “yes, that was like this.” This feels like its own thing. Maybe there were low periods before, but I’m not sure they were the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had another episode of depression like this one before in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t really know. I keep trying to line it up in my head—like, was there another time that lasted more than two weeks and felt like this—and then I end up remembering odd things instead, like where I was living then or what was happening with the neighbor’s dog barking all night.\n\nBut the actual answer… I can’t confidently say yes. I’ve had low patches before, sure, but whether they were this kind of thing, and whether they went on more than two weeks, I just can’t pin it down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. But now I’ll turn the TV on, or even just think about putting a game on, and it feels pointless. Like I can’t get into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt’s strange, because mornings used to be when I’d make coffee and check the paper—well, online now, I guess, though I still miss the actual newspaper—and lately I catch myself thinking, what if I just didn’t have to do another one of these days.\n\nAnd what really scared me was I found myself going through my finances, sort of organizing things, like I was getting it ready for someone else to handle. I don’t know… that felt different. It made me think maybe I shouldn’t just keep this to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think of something, but… not really. I used to look forward to the model railway, you know, fiddling with the little tracks and buildings—there’s one train just sitting there half-built on the table, and I can’t seem to make myself care about finishing it.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch those together, make jokes about the announcers and all that. Now even turning a game on feels… pointless. Like it’s just noise in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I’ll mean to, and then I get distracted or it feels like too much trouble, and suddenly it’s late and I realize I haven’t eaten. Then I’ll just have toast or a bowl of cereal, if that. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this overwhelming sense of failure and hopelessness before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve felt I messed things up before—everybody has, I suppose—but this is different.\n\nIt’s like… I came in because I can’t get away from this feeling that I failed as a father, and failed at being any kind of useful person. It just loops around in my head. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.\n\nIt’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode of depression like this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s hard to separate the actual feeling from all the bits around it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll remember a room I was sitting in, or some stupid errand I kept putting off, and then I lose the thread of what you asked.\n\nBut… no, I can’t point to another time and say, “yes, that was like this.” This feels like its own thing. Maybe there were low periods before, but I’m not sure they were the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had another episode of depression like this one before in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t really know. I keep trying to line it up in my head—like, was there another time that lasted more than two weeks and felt like this—and then I end up remembering odd things instead, like where I was living then or what was happening with the neighbor’s dog barking all night.\n\nBut the actual answer… I can’t confidently say yes. I’ve had low patches before, sure, but whether they were this kind of thing, and whether they went on more than two weeks, I just can’t pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression before this one that a doctor diagnosed or treated?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember clearly. I’ve talked to doctors over the years for ordinary things, you know, blood pressure checks and that sort of stuff, and sometimes you end up mentioning you’re tired or not yourself, but I don’t remember anyone saying, “this is depression,” or giving me treatment for an episode like this.\n\nThen again my memory for appointments is strange—I remember the waiting room chairs better than what was actually said half the time. But no, I can’t point to a previous time where a doctor diagnosed or treated me for this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "Determine if the patient has had a previous episode of depression", "question": "Have you ever been treated for depression before this episode?"}

My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this overwhelming sense of failure and hopelessness before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve felt I messed things up before—everybody has, I suppose—but this is different.\n\nIt’s like… I came in because I can’t get away from this feeling that I failed as a father, and failed at being any kind of useful person. It just loops around in my head. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.\n\nIt’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode of depression like this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s hard to separate the actual feeling from all the bits around it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll remember a room I was sitting in, or some stupid errand I kept putting off, and then I lose the thread of what you asked.\n\nBut… no, I can’t point to another time and say, “yes, that was like this.” This feels like its own thing. Maybe there were low periods before, but I’m not sure they were the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had another episode of depression like this one before in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t really know. I keep trying to line it up in my head—like, was there another time that lasted more than two weeks and felt like this—and then I end up remembering odd things instead, like where I was living then or what was happening with the neighbor’s dog barking all night.\n\nBut the actual answer… I can’t confidently say yes. I’ve had low patches before, sure, but whether they were this kind of thing, and whether they went on more than two weeks, I just can’t pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression before this one that a doctor diagnosed or treated?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember clearly. I’ve talked to doctors over the years for ordinary things, you know, blood pressure checks and that sort of stuff, and sometimes you end up mentioning you’re tired or not yourself, but I don’t remember anyone saying, “this is depression,” or giving me treatment for an episode like this.\n\nThen again my memory for appointments is strange—I remember the waiting room chairs better than what was actually said half the time. But no, I can’t point to a previous time where a doctor diagnosed or treated me for this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been treated for depression before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I know I already sort of said this, but I can’t remember ever being treated for it before… not in any clear way. \n\nI’ve had appointments where other things were going on, and you know how the conversation wanders—at least mine does—and I might’ve said I wasn’t feeling right. But actual treatment for depression before this? No, I don’t have a memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a doctor?\n\nPatient: Right… I think you’re asking the same sort of thing, whether there was some earlier time a doctor treated me for it. And I just don’t have that, not clearly.\n\nI can remember bits around doctor visits—like parking lots, or being annoyed because I’d forgotten a form—but I don’t remember being treated for another episode like this. So… no, not that I know of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. I mean, I remember being in hospitals visiting other people, and those places all sort of blend together—the parking garages, the cafeteria coffee, that smell they all have—but for me being hospitalized for depression before? No, I don’t have a memory of that happening.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I can’t get away from this feeling that I’ve failed. As a father, mostly, and just as a useful person in general. It sort of loops in my head.\n\nI was sitting at home the other day looking at this old toolbox I never use anymore—funny what you notice when you’re just sitting there—and I kept thinking, what good am I to anyone now? And then it goes from there to feeling like maybe I don’t really deserve much of a future. That’s… that’s why I’m here, I think.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about failing as a father and a person?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I knew she was going—of course I knew—but the house got so quiet, and I keep noticing little things she left behind, like a mug or a sweatshirt, and then I start thinking I didn’t do enough, or I somehow messed it all up. So… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway—had this little station I was building, tiny benches and everything—and it would kind of settle me down. Now it’s just sitting there half-finished on the table.\n\nAnd Cubs games too… my daughter and I used to watch them together, even when they were terrible, which, you know, they often are. But now I’ll turn the TV on, or even just think about putting a game on, and it feels pointless. Like I can’t get into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt’s strange, because mornings used to be when I’d make coffee and check the paper—well, online now, I guess, though I still miss the actual newspaper—and lately I catch myself thinking, what if I just didn’t have to do another one of these days.\n\nAnd what really scared me was I found myself going through my finances, sort of organizing things, like I was getting it ready for someone else to handle. I don’t know… that felt different. It made me think maybe I shouldn’t just keep this to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think of something, but… not really. I used to look forward to the model railway, you know, fiddling with the little tracks and buildings—there’s one train just sitting there half-built on the table, and I can’t seem to make myself care about finishing it.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch those together, make jokes about the announcers and all that. Now even turning a game on feels… pointless. Like it’s just noise in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I’ll mean to, and then I get distracted or it feels like too much trouble, and suddenly it’s late and I realize I haven’t eaten. Then I’ll just have toast or a bowl of cereal, if that. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this overwhelming sense of failure and hopelessness before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve felt I messed things up before—everybody has, I suppose—but this is different.\n\nIt’s like… I came in because I can’t get away from this feeling that I failed as a father, and failed at being any kind of useful person. It just loops around in my head. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.\n\nIt’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode of depression like this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s hard to separate the actual feeling from all the bits around it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll remember a room I was sitting in, or some stupid errand I kept putting off, and then I lose the thread of what you asked.\n\nBut… no, I can’t point to another time and say, “yes, that was like this.” This feels like its own thing. Maybe there were low periods before, but I’m not sure they were the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had another episode of depression like this one before in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t really know. I keep trying to line it up in my head—like, was there another time that lasted more than two weeks and felt like this—and then I end up remembering odd things instead, like where I was living then or what was happening with the neighbor’s dog barking all night.\n\nBut the actual answer… I can’t confidently say yes. I’ve had low patches before, sure, but whether they were this kind of thing, and whether they went on more than two weeks, I just can’t pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression before this one that a doctor diagnosed or treated?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember clearly. I’ve talked to doctors over the years for ordinary things, you know, blood pressure checks and that sort of stuff, and sometimes you end up mentioning you’re tired or not yourself, but I don’t remember anyone saying, “this is depression,” or giving me treatment for an episode like this.\n\nThen again my memory for appointments is strange—I remember the waiting room chairs better than what was actually said half the time. But no, I can’t point to a previous time where a doctor diagnosed or treated me for this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been treated for depression before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I know I already sort of said this, but I can’t remember ever being treated for it before… not in any clear way. \n\nI’ve had appointments where other things were going on, and you know how the conversation wanders—at least mine does—and I might’ve said I wasn’t feeling right. But actual treatment for depression before this? No, I don’t have a memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a doctor?\n\nPatient: Right… I think you’re asking the same sort of thing, whether there was some earlier time a doctor treated me for it. And I just don’t have that, not clearly.\n\nI can remember bits around doctor visits—like parking lots, or being annoyed because I’d forgotten a form—but I don’t remember being treated for another episode like this. So… no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been hospitalized for depression before?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I remember being in hospitals visiting other people, and those places all sort of blend together—the parking garages, the cafeteria coffee, that smell they all have—but for me being hospitalized for depression before? No, I don’t have a memory of that happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Major Depressive Disorder. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came because I can’t get away from this feeling that I’ve failed. As a father, mostly, and just as a useful person in general. It sort of loops in my head.\n\nI was sitting at home the other day looking at this old toolbox I never use anymore—funny what you notice when you’re just sitting there—and I kept thinking, what good am I to anyone now? And then it goes from there to feeling like maybe I don’t really deserve much of a future. That’s… that’s why I’m here, I think.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about failing as a father and a person?\n\nPatient: About a month, I think. It really started after my daughter left for college out of state. I mean, I knew she was going—of course I knew—but the house got so quiet, and I keep noticing little things she left behind, like a mug or a sweatshirt, and then I start thinking I didn’t do enough, or I somehow messed it all up. So… yeah, about a month.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself enjoying things you typically like to do over the past month?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I used to really look forward to working on my model railway—had this little station I was building, tiny benches and everything—and it would kind of settle me down. Now it’s just sitting there half-finished on the table.\n\nAnd Cubs games too… my daughter and I used to watch them together, even when they were terrible, which, you know, they often are. But now I’ll turn the TV on, or even just think about putting a game on, and it feels pointless. Like I can’t get into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I’ve made some big plan exactly, but I have had thoughts like… it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up.\n\nIt’s strange, because mornings used to be when I’d make coffee and check the paper—well, online now, I guess, though I still miss the actual newspaper—and lately I catch myself thinking, what if I just didn’t have to do another one of these days.\n\nAnd what really scared me was I found myself going through my finances, sort of organizing things, like I was getting it ready for someone else to handle. I don’t know… that felt different. It made me think maybe I shouldn’t just keep this to myself.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in or look forward to?\n\nPatient: I keep trying to think of something, but… not really. I used to look forward to the model railway, you know, fiddling with the little tracks and buildings—there’s one train just sitting there half-built on the table, and I can’t seem to make myself care about finishing it.\n\nAnd Cubs games, too. My daughter and I used to watch those together, make jokes about the announcers and all that. Now even turning a game on feels… pointless. Like it’s just noise in the room.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past month?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I haven’t weighed myself properly, but my belt’s definitely on a tighter notch now.\n\nMost days I just… don’t bother making a real meal. I’ll mean to, and then I get distracted or it feels like too much trouble, and suddenly it’s late and I realize I haven’t eaten. Then I’ll just have toast or a bowl of cereal, if that. I used to actually cook sometimes—nothing fancy, but still—and now it’s like the whole process feels pointless.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this way before, like you're failing and don't deserve a future, in the past before this month?\n\nPatient: I… before this month? I don’t think it’s ever been quite like this. I mean, I’ve had rough stretches where I felt like I wasn’t doing enough—most parents probably feel that, right? My father used to say you just keep your head down and do what needs doing, and I guess I tried to live that way.\n\nBut lately it’s been… more fixed. Like this loop in my head that I failed as a father, and failed at being useful at all. I came in because I can’t shake that feeling, that I don’t really deserve much of a future. And then I’ll start thinking about little things I should’ve done differently, conversations with my daughter years ago, stuff that probably doesn’t even matter the way I’m making it matter, but it just keeps going around and around.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this overwhelming sense of failure and hopelessness before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve felt I messed things up before—everybody has, I suppose—but this is different.\n\nIt’s like… I came in because I can’t get away from this feeling that I failed as a father, and failed at being any kind of useful person. It just loops around in my head. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.\n\nIt’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode of depression like this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s hard to separate the actual feeling from all the bits around it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll remember a room I was sitting in, or some stupid errand I kept putting off, and then I lose the thread of what you asked.\n\nBut… no, I can’t point to another time and say, “yes, that was like this.” This feels like its own thing. Maybe there were low periods before, but I’m not sure they were the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had another episode of depression like this one before in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t really know. I keep trying to line it up in my head—like, was there another time that lasted more than two weeks and felt like this—and then I end up remembering odd things instead, like where I was living then or what was happening with the neighbor’s dog barking all night.\n\nBut the actual answer… I can’t confidently say yes. I’ve had low patches before, sure, but whether they were this kind of thing, and whether they went on more than two weeks, I just can’t pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression before this one that a doctor diagnosed or treated?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember clearly. I’ve talked to doctors over the years for ordinary things, you know, blood pressure checks and that sort of stuff, and sometimes you end up mentioning you’re tired or not yourself, but I don’t remember anyone saying, “this is depression,” or giving me treatment for an episode like this.\n\nThen again my memory for appointments is strange—I remember the waiting room chairs better than what was actually said half the time. But no, I can’t point to a previous time where a doctor diagnosed or treated me for this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been treated for depression before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I know I already sort of said this, but I can’t remember ever being treated for it before… not in any clear way. \n\nI’ve had appointments where other things were going on, and you know how the conversation wanders—at least mine does—and I might’ve said I wasn’t feeling right. But actual treatment for depression before this? No, I don’t have a memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a doctor?\n\nPatient: Right… I think you’re asking the same sort of thing, whether there was some earlier time a doctor treated me for it. So I guess… not that I can really identify.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll be making tea or looking at some old thing on the shelf, and suddenly I’m back thinking about what I should’ve done differently, and then it turns into this feeling that I don’t really deserve much of a future.\n\nSorry, I’m rambling. But no, before this past month, I don’t remember it being this overwhelming.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with depression before?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not formally, anyway.\n\nI mean, I’ve had times where people said I seemed “down” or that I should talk to someone, but that’s different, isn’t it? My wife—well, she used to have this way of saying I’d go quiet and disappear into myself, usually around stressful times. But no, I don’t remember a doctor ever sitting me down and saying, yes, that’s what it is.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: More than two weeks… yes, I suppose this time it has. It’s been about a month now, really—since my daughter left for college out of state.\n\nI keep thinking it shouldn’t have hit me this hard. People’s kids leave home all the time, don’t they? I remember helping carry boxes, and there was this one lamp she insisted on taking, even though I told her the dorm probably already had lights… sorry. But yes, about a month. This has been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt this low or depressed before in your life, prior to the past month?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not this low. I’ve had bad stretches, of course. I remember after my father got ill I was sort of walking around with this weight on me, and I kept fixing things in the house that didn’t really need fixing—there was a cupboard door I must’ve adjusted three times—because if I sat still, I’d start thinking too much.\n\nBut even then, I don’t remember it feeling like this. This feels… heavier, I suppose. Harder to climb out of. So, no, I don’t think I’ve been this low before.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to count it, honestly. There were… stretches, I suppose, where I was low for a while, but I don’t know if they were more than two weeks or just felt long because the days were dragging.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little details better than the feeling itself—like during one bad patch I kept leaving the mail on the hall table until it made this ridiculous pile, and then I’d get annoyed at the pile like it had done something wrong. But whether that was two weeks, or one week, or three… I don’t really know.\n\nSo I can’t say clearly that there’s been more than one. This one, yes. Before that, I’m less certain.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt as depressed as you do now?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I have, no. I mean, I’ve had times where I was down, but as depressed as this… I keep trying to compare it and it gets a bit muddled.\n\nIt’s like when you try to remember if a winter was colder than another winter, and then suddenly you’re thinking about the boiler breaking or what coat you were wearing instead of the actual cold. So… I can’t really say there’s been another time like this. This feels different, heavier.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced an episode of depression like this one before in your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I mean, I’ve been trying to think back, but it’s hard to separate the actual feeling from all the bits around it, if that makes sense. Like I’ll remember a room I was sitting in, or some stupid errand I kept putting off, and then I lose the thread of what you asked.\n\nBut… no, I can’t point to another time and say, “yes, that was like this.” This feels like its own thing. Maybe there were low periods before, but I’m not sure they were the same as this.\n\nDoctor: Have you had another episode of depression like this one before in your life that lasted for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I still don’t really know. I keep trying to line it up in my head—like, was there another time that lasted more than two weeks and felt like this—and then I end up remembering odd things instead, like where I was living then or what was happening with the neighbor’s dog barking all night.\n\nBut the actual answer… I can’t confidently say yes. I’ve had low patches before, sure, but whether they were this kind of thing, and whether they went on more than two weeks, I just can’t pin it down.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had an episode of depression before this one that a doctor diagnosed or treated?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not that I can remember clearly. I’ve talked to doctors over the years for ordinary things, you know, blood pressure checks and that sort of stuff, and sometimes you end up mentioning you’re tired or not yourself, but I don’t remember anyone saying, “this is depression,” or giving me treatment for an episode like this.\n\nThen again my memory for appointments is strange—I remember the waiting room chairs better than what was actually said half the time. But no, I can’t point to a previous time where a doctor diagnosed or treated me for this.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been treated for depression before this episode?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I know I already sort of said this, but I can’t remember ever being treated for it before… not in any clear way. \n\nI’ve had appointments where other things were going on, and you know how the conversation wanders—at least mine does—and I might’ve said I wasn’t feeling right. But actual treatment for depression before this? No, I don’t have a memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any other episodes of depression in your life that were treated by a doctor?\n\nPatient: Right… I think you’re asking the same sort of thing, whether there was some earlier time a doctor treated me for it. And I just don’t have that, not clearly.\n\nI can remember bits around doctor visits—like parking lots, or being annoyed because I’d forgotten a form—but I don’t remember being treated for another episode like this. So… no, not that I know of.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been hospitalized for depression before?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I remember being in hospitals visiting other people, and those places all sort of blend together—the parking garages, the cafeteria coffee, that smell they all have—but for me being hospitalized for depression before? No, I don’t have a memory of that happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any previous episodes of depression that were similar to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I’ve had bad stretches in life, like anyone, but whether they were really like this… I can’t say clearly.\n\nIt’s funny, I can remember little things from years ago—what apartment I was living in, this old coffee mug I used every morning—but I don’t have a clear memory of an episode that matches this one. So I guess… not that I can really identify."
